Records from the S-Matrix Marathon: Asymptotic Observables: The Analytic S-Matrix Revisited
Lecturers, Simon Caron-Huot, Mathieu Giroux, Notes written by, Mathieu Giroux, Holmfridur S. Hannesdottir, Sebastian Mizera, Celina Pasiecznik

Abstract
These lectures introduce the notion of asymptotic observables , which are classes of measurable quantities predicted by quantum field theory. In gapped theories with trivial infrared dynamics, these include scattering amplitudes, expectation values of operators approaching infinity, inclusive cross sections, etc. We argue for a unified picture in which various asymptotic observables are related by analytic continuation embodying new versions of crossing symmetry. As an application, we discuss the exponentiation of infrared divergences for inclusive observables in Quantum Electrodynamics using time-folded contours. Finally, we outline prospects for a systematic study of analyticity properties of asymptotic observables using the Fourier–Bros–Iagolnitzer transform. These notes are based on a series of lectures held during the S-Matrix Marathon workshop at the Institute for Advanced Study on 11–22 March 2024.



Preface

This article is a chapter from the
Records from the S-Matrix Marathon
, a series of lecture notes covering selected topics on scattering amplitudes
[
1
]
. They are based on lectures delivered during a workshop on 11–22 March 2024 at the Institute for Advanced Study in Princeton, NJ. We hope that they can serve as a pedagogical introduction to the topics surrounding the S-matrix theory.

These lecture notes were prepared by the above-mentioned note-writers in collaboration with the lecturers.

Asymptotic Observables: The Analytic S-Matrix Revisited

Simon Caron-Huot, Mathieu Giroux

Contents

1
Asymptotic observables and crossing

Simon Caron-Huot

In this first lecture, we introduce a minimal set of axioms that allow us to enumerate asymptotic observables of a given multiplicity. We will then explain why these are, remarkably, interconnected through crossing symmetry. The main references on which these lectures are based are
[
2
,
3
]
.

1.1
Summary of conventions

First, we spell out here the conventions assumed in this chapter, which follow
[
3
]
. Unless specified otherwise, we work in
\mathrm{D}
spacetime dimensions and take the momentum components of
p^{\mu}
to be

p^{\mu}=(p^{0},\underbracket{p^{1},\ldots,p^{\mathrm{D}-1}}_{=\mathbf{p}})\,.

(1)

We work in mostly-plus signature such that
p^{2}=-(p^{0})^{2}+(p^{1})^{2}+\ldots+(p^{\mathrm{D}-1})^{2}
and all-outgoing conventions, in which
p_{i}^{0}<0
for the incoming particles and
p_{i}^{0}>0
for the outgoing ones. When using lightcone coordinates, we define

p^{\pm}=p^{0}\pm p^{\mathrm{D}-1}\,,\quad\mathbf{p}^{\perp}=(p^{1},\ldots,p^{\mathrm{D}-2})\,,

(2)

such that
p^{2}=-p^{+}p^{-}+(\mathbf{p}^{\perp})^{2}
.
Furthermore, we use the notation
p_{ij\cdots k}^{\mu}=p_{i}^{\mu}+p_{j}^{\mu}+\ldots+p_{k}^{\mu}
, and
s_{ij\cdots k}=-p_{ij\cdots k}^{2}
for Mandelstam invariants.

In what follows, the
connected interacting part

T
of the S-matrix is obtained in the usual way:

S=\mathbbm{1}+iT+\ldots\,,

(3)

where “
\ldots
” include disconnected products of
T
’s and are only present for multiplicities greater than
2\leftarrow 2
.
The interacting part of the
scattering amplitude
,
\mathcal{M}
, is defined as the matrix element of
T
with the overall momentum-conserving delta function stripped out:

\mathcal{M}_{f\leftarrow i}=\langle f|T|i\rangle(2\pi)^{\mathrm{D}}\delta^{\mathrm{D}}\Big(\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\mu}\Big)\,.

(4)

When drawing diagrams, we will follow the operator ordering in bra-ket notation (see, e.g.,
eqs.

23
,
25a
and
25b
below).
This means (thanks Dirac!) that time flows toward the
left
in scattering amplitudes
S
or
\mathcal{M}
,
and toward the right in conjugated amplitudes
S^{\dagger}
or
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
factors.

Finally, “in” scattering states are denoted without explicit labels:
\bra{...}={}_{\text{in}}\bra{...}
and
\ket{...}=\ket{...}_{\text{in}}
. In contrast, the “out” states will remain labeled:
{}_{\text{out}}\bra{...}
and
\ket{...}_{\text{out}}
.

1.2
Asymptotic observables

The discussion that follows is motivated by the observation that, in local quantum field theory, causality admits a precise statement in terms of
microcausality
. It asserts that operators corresponding to observables at spacelike separated points must commute:

[O(x),O(y)]=0\qquad\text{(spacelike)}\,,

(5)

ensuring that it is impossible to send signals faster than light.

Vacuum matrix elements of such relations,

\bra{0}[\Phi(y),\Phi^{\dagger}(x)]\ket{0}=0

are well known to imply that any particle must have an antiparticle of the same mass and spin. That is, the two terms in the commutator can only cancel out
if the amplitude for a
\Phi
particle to move from
x
to
y
(which is nonvanishing spacelike) coincides with that of a corresponding antiparticle to go from
y
to
x
:

t
\mathbf{x}
x
y
particle
anti-particle

(6)

The question we wish to explore below is the following: What are the implications of (
5
) inside a general scattering state?
As we shall see, this will relate the amplitudes of particles and those of antiparticles:
crossing symmetry
, but the answer will also reveal new and non-trivial relations among
out-of-time-order
(OTO) observables. The OTO nature of crossing can already be anticipated, since commutators alter the ordering of the operators.

In time-ordered perturbation theory, the relation (
5
) (and the analogous anti-commutation relation for fermions) ensures Lorentz invariance of T-products
[
4
]
. Here we will directly use (
5
), which seems to buy us more mileage at the end of the day.

Next, let us outline the assumptions used to make this discussion more precise and to derive the results below.

1.2.1
S-matrix axioms

The idealized setup that epitomizes the idea of the S-matrix is a quantum field theory (QFT) with trivial infrared dynamics (e.g., pion scattering in
\mathrm{D}=4
, QED and QCD in dimensional regularization, etc.). In this setup, it makes sense to
assume
that any finite energy excitation decays at late times into a finite set of stable particles. These particles then separate from each other and effectively become free as they cease to overlap. This assumption and its consequences are encoded in the following set of axioms
[
3
]
(similar sets of axioms have a long history
[
5
]
):

(i)

The algebra of asymptotic measurements in the
far past
is generated by
creation
and
annihilation operators
of stable particles,
satisfying the canonical relation:

[a_{i},a^{\dagger}_{j}]=\delta_{i,j}\ 2p_{i}^{0}\ (2\pi)^{\mathrm{D}-1}\delta^{\mathrm{D}-1}(\mathbf{p}_{i}-\mathbf{p}_{j})~~\text{and}~~[a_{i},a_{j}]=[a_{i}^{\dagger},a^{\dagger}_{j}]=0\,.

(7)

Here,
p_{i}^{0}
is the (positive) energy of the
i^{\text{th}}
particle and

\delta_{i,j}
denotes a Kronecker delta in the flavor and spin indices.

(i’)

There is an equivalent algebra of “out” measurements in the

far future
, which we denote by
b
and
b^{\dagger}
’s.
Translated to textbook conventions, the above creation and annihilation operators correspond to

a_{i}\equiv a_{i}^{\mathrm{in}}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{i}\equiv a_{i}^{\mathrm{out}}\,.

(8)

(ii)

These operators act on equivalent Hilbert spaces and are related by a unitary
evolution operator

S
:

b_{i}=S^{\dagger}a_{i}S,\qquad b_{i}^{\dagger}=S^{\dagger}a_{i}^{\dagger}S\,.

(9)

It follows from (
9
) that
[a,b]
can be a complicated object. This is expected because measurements in the past affect the future in a complicated way.

(iii)

There exists a
time-invariant vacuum
,
|0\rangle
, that does not contain particles:

a_{i}|0\rangle=b_{i}|0\rangle=0,\qquad S|0\rangle=|0\rangle\,.

(10)

(iv)

One-particle states evolve trivially, which means that they are
stable

b^{\dagger}_{i}|0\rangle=S^{\dagger}a^{\dagger}_{i}|0\rangle=a^{\dagger}_{i}|0\rangle\,.

(11)

This also implies
Sa_{i}^{\dagger}|0\rangle=a_{i}^{\dagger}|0\rangle
from the unitarity condition
S^{\dagger}S=\mathbbm{1}
.

Given these axioms and the algebra of measurements, we can now ask:
what can be measured asymptotically?
We organize the discussion by multiplicity, starting at four-point.

1.2.2
Enumerating observables:
four-point

The possible four-point measurements are of the form

\langle 0|...|0\rangle\quad\text{where ``...'' is any length-four string of}~a,a^{\dagger},b,b^{\dagger}\,.

(12)

Naturally, numerous of these combinations result in trivial observables. This is because
a\ket{0}=b\ket{0}=0
, and both
a_{i}a_{j}^{\dagger}\ket{0}
and
b_{i}b_{j}^{\dagger}\ket{0}
are proportional to a delta function, which vanishes for non-forward momenta (and in any rate is a
c
-number times

\ket{0}
according to (
7
): an uninteresting state!).
Therefore, only two non-trivial states can be generated by acting with two asymptotic operators on the vacuum:

a_{i}^{\dagger}a_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle\equiv\ket{ij}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{i}^{\dagger}a_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle=b_{i}^{\dagger}b_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle\equiv\ket{ij}_{\text{out}}\,.

(13)

These are the
only
two options! Multiplying by the analogous classification on the left, we conclude that the possible observables are therefore the conventional (causal) scattering amplitude

\begin{split}\langle 0|b_{4}b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle&=\langle 0|a_{4}a_{3}Sa^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle=\bra{34}S\ket{12}={}_{\text{out}}\braket{34|12}\,,\end{split}

(14)

the Hermitian conjugated (anti-causal) scattering amplitude

\begin{split}\langle 0|a_{4}a_{3}b^{\dagger}_{2}b^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle&=\langle 0|a_{4}a_{3}S^{\dagger}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle=\bra{34}S^{\dagger}\ket{12}\,,\end{split}

(15)

and the forwards terms

\begin{split}\langle 0|a_{4}a_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle&=\bra{34}\mathbbm{1}\ket{12}=\delta_{12,34}=\langle 0|b_{4}b_{3}b^{\dagger}_{2}b^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle\,.\end{split}

(16)

Restricting two non-forward kinematics, we find only
two
four-point observables: (
14
) and (
15
). Given the age of this subject, it is reassuring that we did not discover anything new at four points.
The fact that there are two observables and not just a single one is significant.
As we will elaborate on later, crossing symmetry relates these two objects—amplitudes to their Hermitian conjugate, and vice versa!

s
\mathcal{M}_{1\bar{3}\to\bar{2}4}^{\dagger}
\mathcal{M}_{12\to 34}

(17)

Before moving on, let us stress that crossing is not simply a matter of “flipping the sign of energies in formulas”. Consider for example the integrated expression for an
s
-channel massless bubble integral in minimal subtraction:

{\rm Bub}(s)\propto\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\log\frac{-s}{\mu^{2}}&s<0\\
\log\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}-i\pi&s>0\end{array}\right.\,.

(18)

To go from one expression to the other, we have to follow a precise
continuation path
. The arc in the upper-half
s
-plane tells us exactly the correct branch choice.

1.2.3
Enumerating observables:
five-point

As should now be clear, the task of cataloging all possible observables is a combinatorial exercise. Hence, as the number of points increases, we should encounter increasingly non-trivial possibilities, leading to more exotic observables beyond the conventional scattering amplitudes.
This becomes manifest already for five points. For example, while we still have the
3\leftarrow 2
scattering amplitudes

\langle 0|b_{5}b_{4}b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle=\bra{345}S\ket{12}\,,

(19)

we also have observables of the form

\langle 0|a_{5}a_{4}b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle=\bra{45}b_{3}\ket{12}=\bra{45}S^{\dagger}a_{3}S\ket{12}\,.

(20)

As the writing makes manifest, (
20
) computes an expectation value for observing particle 3 in a particular
in
state that contains two particles, and is totally agnostic as to what happens to these particles
after
particle 3 is emitted. (Both the bra and ket are
in
states: no future boundary conditions are imposed.)
We can make (
20
) look more like a conventional quantum-mechanical expectation value by integrating the on-shell momenta
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
against a wavepacket:

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{p_{1},p_{2}}\psi(p_{1},p_{2})\ket{12}

(21)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,\,{}_{\text{in}}\!\bra{\psi}b_{3}\ket{\psi}_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle=\int_{p_{1},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}}\psi^{*}(p_{4},p_{5})\psi(p_{1},p_{2})\bra{45}b_{3}\ket{12}\,.

Note that (
21
) is
not
measuring the type 3 particle number; such an observable would be quadratic in field 3. Instead, (
21
) measures the linear (leading order) response of the scattering background in field 3.
In which realistic situations could this arise? For example, (
21
) could represent (i) the expectation value of the electromagnetic field associated with radiation after the collision of two charged particles (further discussed in Sec.
2.3
), or (ii) the expectation value of the graviton field (gravitational wave signal) generated by the scattering of two massive objects (e.g., black holes), which is further discussed in Sec.
1.3
. Both are examples of radiative
waveforms
.
In this context, the wavefunction
\psi
sets the initial energy and impact parameter of the colliding bodies (see
[
6
]
).

Let us reiterate a basic but important point: the signal observed at a detector such as LIGO/VIRGO/KAGRA is
linear
in metric perturbations.
Linear measurements of electric fields, while not necessarily important nor natural at colliders, are familiar in low-energy physics: this is what a voltmeter measures.
Although the definition (
21
) makes no reference to coherence effects,
such linear measurements are typically only practical when the final state contains a coherent state of many photons or gravitons that makes the expectation value (
21
) large.

That said, it may not yet be clear from (
21
)
how
the observable should be computed in practice (e.g., using perturbation theory). To get started, it is useful to insert in (
20
) a complete basis of on-shell states

\mathbbm{1}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\ket{X}_{\mathrlap{\text{in}}}{\hskip-12.80365pt\phantom{\bra{X}}}{}_{\mathrlap{\text{in}}\hskip 4.26773pt}\bra{X}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\ket{X}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}}{\hskip-5.69046pt\phantom{\bra{X}}}{}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}\hskip 9.95863pt}\bra{X}\,,

(22)

as follows:

\begin{split}\langle 0|a_{5}a_{4}b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle&=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\bra{45}S^{\dagger}\ket{X}\bra{X3}S\ket{12}\\
&=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

(23)

To be clear, (
22
) is a sum over the complete basis of states
\ket{X}\bra{X}
integrated over the
on-shell phase space

\hskip-11.38092pt\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\ket{X}\bra{X}{=}\int\frac{\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}-1}p_{1}}{(2\pi)^{\mathrm{D}-1}2E_{p_{1}}}\ket{p_{1}}\bra{p_{1}}{+}\int\prod_{j=1}^{2}\frac{\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}-1}p_{j}}{(2\pi)^{\mathrm{D}-1}2E_{p_{j}}}\frac{\ket{p_{1}p_{2}}\bra{p_{1}p_{2}}}{2!}{+}\ldots

(24)

Now, from (
23
), one way to practically compute the waveform observable becomes apparent: it is the product of S-matrix elements (each computable separately using e.g. Feynman diagrams) connected by an on-shell phase-space integral.
Note that this calculation is completely inclusive in the final state
X
:
as far as we care, the colliding bodies could annihilate each other and we would still get a non-trivial waveform!

All other observables at five-point are either forward or obtained by Hermitian conjugation or time reversal of those listed above. Thus, we move on to six-point.

1.2.4
Enumerating observables:
six-point

Similar to the previous instances, conventional six-point scattering amplitudes (i.e.,
\bra{3456}S\ket{12}
,
\bra{456}S\ket{123}
, relevant for scattering of one parton against two partons and their Hermitian conjugates) remain. More intriguingly, we have observables of the form

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{53.90433pt}{-3.32494pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 74.59 -4.6)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}}}$}\,.

(25b)

In the forward limit (
6,5,4\to 1,2,3
), the observable (
25a
) is well known: it is the inclusive cross-section measuring the number of particles of type 3 produced in the scattering of particles 1 and 2:
\langle 0|a_{6}a_{5}b^{\dagger}_{4}b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle\to\bra{12}N_{3}\ket{12}
, with
N_{3}=b^{\dagger}_{3}b_{3}
quadratic in the field.

The same observable (
25a
) also appears in more exotic situations. For center-of-mass energies well above the Planck mass
\sqrt{s_{12}}\gg M_{\text{pl}}
, one could imagine (
25a
) as measuring the “square” of the following process:

BH
2
1
3
…
\mathcal{O}(S)~\text{states}

(26)

where particles 1 and 2 collide and form a black hole, which eventually fully evaporates by emitting Hawking radiation. In a strict
S
-matrix approach, the exact final state is a complicated combination of
\mathcal{O}(S)
quanta where
S
is the entropy of the intermediate black hole, with matrix elements that are typically exponentially small
\sim\mathrm{e}^{-S/2}
by general statistical considerations. The calculation of this quantum state remains an unsolved task, even in principle (although there was notable recent progress in understanding its statistical properties using replica wormholes).
However, (
25a
) is a much simpler object because it is agnostic about the complicated cloud of on-shell final states and only cares about the particular Hawking quantum labeled
3
in the figure.
(By making the momenta
p_{3}
and
p_{4}
slightly non-forward and performing a Wigner transform, the observable is also sensitive to the arrival time of the measured radiation.)
Viewing (
25a
) as a two-point function in a black hole geometry also makes it clear that it can be reliably calculated without knowing the exact final state—in fact this is exactly how Hawking
[
7
]
calculated it!

Let us finally comment on (
25b
), which is at face value a “maximal scrambling” of
a
’s and
b
’s. It is useful to consider particles 1 and 6 as defining a background state and to interpret this observable as a
four-point
OTOC (commutator squared) in this background. By transforming to the time domain and applying a large-time translation to
b^{\dagger}_{3}
and
b_{5}
, this process potentially probes the Lyapunov exponent characterizing the chaotic growth of small perturbation
b^{\dagger}_{3}
(see
[
8
]
for more references).
For a more complete list of six-point observables, see
[
2
]
.

1.3
(Some) crossing moves

We now sketch how crossing symmetry bridges some of the above observables through analytic continuations in the kinematic space.
The basic object (further discussed in Sec.
2.1
) is the retarded product of currents
j\propto(-\partial^{2}+m^{2})\phi
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}(p_{2},p_{3})=\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{2}\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{3}\mathrm{e}^{-i(p_{2}\cdot x_{2}+p_{3}\cdot x_{3})}{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{B}R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})\ket{A}\,,\end{split}

(27)

where
R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})=[j_{3},j_{2}]\theta(x_{3}^{0}{>}x_{2}^{0})
denotes the retarded product.
The current
j
amputates the external propagators and following the logic of the LSZ reduction formula one can show that for real on-shell momenta :

\mathcal{R}(p_{2},p_{3})=\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\bra{B}[b_{3},a^{\dagger}_{2}]\ket{A}&p_{2}^{0}<0\,,~p_{3}^{0}>0\,,\\
-\bra{B}[a_{2},b^{\dagger}_{3}]\ket{A}&p_{2}^{0}>0\,,~p_{3}^{0}<0\,.\\
\end{array}\right.~~\text{(on-shell)}

(28)

The challenge of crossing is to parameterize a
continuous
path of analytic continuation that (i) is wholly supported on the mass shell and (ii) connects the
two preceding real configurations (
28
):

z
\bra{B}[b_{3},a^{\dagger}_{2}]\ket{A}
-\bra{B}[a_{2},b^{\dagger}_{3}]\ket{A}
\mathcal{R}(p_{2},p_{3})
?
?

(29)

In the case of gapped (and infrared-trivial) quantum field theories, such a path is guaranteed to exist for
2\leftarrow 2
and
3\leftarrow 2
scattering thanks to the work of Bros, Epstein and Glaser initiated in the 1960’s
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
. In gapless theories and at higher multiplicities, the existence of such a path remains conjectural to this day, although recent progress in this direction has been made
[
13
,
3
]
. The
z
-path we will consider is simply a complex boost applied to two of the external momenta; see
[
3
]
for details.

To develop some intuition about why the mass gap plays an important role
without going into the technical details of
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
, we can consider the following example.

{mdexample}

(Intuition for analyticity)

Under the change of variables
x_{3}\mapsto x_{2}+\Delta x
, (
27
) becomes

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}(p_{2},p_{3})=\int_{\Delta x\in\bar{V}^{+}}\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}\Delta x\,\mathrm{e}^{-ip_{3}\cdot\Delta x}\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{3}\mathrm{e}^{-i(p_{2}+p_{3})\cdot x_{2}}{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{B}R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})\ket{A}\,.\end{split}

(30)

The Fourier transform converges provided that (i)
\text{Re}(-ip_{3}\cdot\Delta x)\leqslant 0

\forall\Delta x
and (ii)
(p_{2}+p_{3})^{\mu}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
.
Since the retarded product is supported in the (closure of) the
foward lightcone
\Delta x\in\bar{V}^{+}
, we conclude that the integral is analytic
for
{\rm Im}\,p_{3}^{\mu}\in V^{+}
since then the Fourier transform converges exponentially.
This is the basic reason why we expect analyticity in some upper-half plane.

The difficulty is that a timelike imaginary part for
p_{3}^{\mu}

is incompatible with the mass-shell condition
p_{3}^{2}+m^{2}=0
. Consider for example the on-shell momentum

p_{3}^{\mu}=E(1,v,0,0)+i\varepsilon(v,1,0,0)\qquad(0<v<1\,{\rm real},~E\gg\varepsilon>0)\,.

(31)

The imaginary part is spacelike and point (i) is only satisfied if
\Delta x^{1}<v\Delta t
, where
\Delta t=(x_{3}-x_{2})^{0}>0
and
\Delta x^{1}=(x_{3}-x_{2})^{1}
. Thus, we have a decaying exponential only for displacements
\Delta x

inside
the shaded region:

\Delta x
\Delta t
v\Delta t

(32)

We see that while we got analyticity in
V^{+}
just from the causal support
|\Delta x^{1}|<\Delta t
of the retarded product, analyticity on-shell will only hold if the retarded product is supported within the smaller shaded region
|\Delta x^{1}|<v\Delta t
.
Physically, this is a constraint on the velocity of the intermediate particles produced.

This naive analysis suggests that analyticity is safe for sufficiently boosted external momenta in gapped theories, but can fail in gapless theories whenever
external
particles move more slowly than internal ones.

We hope that this intuition can be formalized in the near future using the concept of
essential support
, described in Sec.
3
below.
For now, it remains unproven whether the domain of analyticity of (
27
) intersects with the mass shell, in general. We will proceed under the assumption that it does.

{mdexample}

(Five-point tree-level with massive resonances)

We now borrow a five-point example from
[
3
]
to illustrate how the inclusive observable (
23
) (equivalent to a product of conventional (time-ordered) amplitudes) is related to a five-point scattering amplitude through analytical continuation. Here, we work at tree level, which is (perhaps surprisingly) sufficient to highlight the non-trivial conceptual aspects of this exercise without the unnecessary complications arising from branch cuts.

Consider a cubic theory where the following tree-level process involving a heavy intermediate particle of mass
M
is possible

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}}}$}\end{gathered}=\frac{-ig^{3}}{(-s_{45}+M^{2}-i\varepsilon)(-s_{13}+M^{2})}\,.

(33)

Note that the
i\varepsilon
only matters if it is kinematically possible to produce
M
, in which case it is necessarily unstable (“if it can be produced, it can decay”). The tree-level diagrams here should be understood as a narrow-width approximation to this resonance.

In (
33
) we dropped the
i\varepsilon
prescription for
s_{13}
since this invariant is spacelike (
s_{13}<0
) in the kinematics under consideration and therefore its propagator cannot go on shell. Nevertheless, the
i\varepsilon
must be retained for the timelike Mandelstam invariant
s_{45}
. We proceed now to analytically continue to the kinematic region where the energies of particles 2 and 3 flip signs. The invariant
s_{13}
, initially negative, rotates in the counterclockwise direction, ending its journey below the positive real axis, while the invariant
s_{45}
remains unchanged:

s_{ij}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s_{45}}
{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}s_{13}}

(34)

The result of this analytic continuation is written as

\left[i\mathcal{M}_{345\leftarrow 12}\right]_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\curvearrowright$}}s_{13}}=\frac{-ig^{3}}{(-s_{45}+M^{2}-i\varepsilon)(-s_{13}+M^{2}+i\varepsilon)}\,,

(35)

where now
s_{13}>0
and its
-i\varepsilon
prescription matters.
Observe that the second propagator acquires the “wrong”
i\varepsilon
prescription. This detail is important because after the crossing we are now in
245\leftarrow 13
kinematics where this pole is accessible.
The fact that
s_{13}
is on the “wrong side” of its cut mirrors what happens for
2\leftarrow 2
scattering, where the large arc (see (
29
)) lands us on the
wrong side of the
u
-channel cut. However, here the
s_{45}
pole remained on the correct side, preventing identifying the right-hand side with a complex conjugated amplitude.
It is a different object
!

Our claim now is that the blob pattern in (
23
) is a shortcut to produce the same result as the analytic continuation we just went through. To see this, substitute
S^{\dagger}=\mathbbm{1}-iT^{\dagger}
and
S=\mathbbm{1}+iT\to iT
into (
23
); for
S
we could drop the
\mathbbm{1}
part since 1 and 2 must interact in order that 3 be emitted, but for
S^{\dagger}
we have both a disconnected and connected term.
They give respectively the complex-conjugated five-point amplitude, plus a non-linear term where the heavy particle
M
is produced:

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\left[i\mathcal{M}_{345\leftarrow 12}\right]_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\curvearrowright$}}s_{13}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}i\mathcal{M}^{{\dagger}}_{245\leftarrow 13}+[i\mathcal{M}_{45\leftarrow M}]\,2\pi\delta(-s_{45}+M^{2})\,[i\mathcal{M}^{{\dagger}}_{M2\leftarrow 13}]

(36a)

\displaystyle=\frac{-ig^{3}}{(-s_{45}+M^{2}+i\varepsilon)(-s_{13}+M^{2}+i\varepsilon)}+\frac{2\pi\delta(-s_{45}+M^{2})g^{3}}{-s_{13}+M^{2}+i\varepsilon}

(36b)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{-s_{45}+M^{2}+i\varepsilon}+2\pi i\delta(-s_{45}+M^{2})\right)\frac{-ig^{3}}{-s_{13}+M^{2}+i\varepsilon}\,.

(36c)

Using the identity
\frac{1}{x\pm i\varepsilon}=\text{PV}\frac{1}{x}\mp i\pi\delta(x)
, the result manifestly agrees with (
35
)! Diagrammatically, we can summarize the discussion as follows:

\left[\begin{gathered}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\hbox to92.5pt{\vbox to44.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.6646pt\lower-28.67152pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}

{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}

{{}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 27.56 0 L 51.43 13.78}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 48.67 -17.71 L 24.8 -31.49}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.84209pt}{-25.33852pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 13.62 -35.06)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 48.67 -17.71 L 72.54 -31.49}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

(37)

Similar computations at loop level are discussed in
[
3
]
, where a general diagrammatic statement of (
29
) is also given.
In particular, we found that the type of computation described above worked for purely massless scattering (without a mass gap). However, we also point out counterexamples in cases where external particles move slower than internal particles, verifying the intuition developed below (
32
).

In the next lecture, we will show how the observables introduced above follow from the reduction of (O)TO products of on-shell currents.

2
Analyticity and IR properties of timefolded observables

Mathieu Giroux

The purpose of this lecture is to revisit the application of reduction formulae for observables with unconventional time ordering through explicit examples. As motivation, we quickly revisit the so-called Lehmann–Symanzik–Zimmermann (LSZ) reduction formula
[
14
]
—which relates conventional time-ordered scattering amplitudes to time-ordered correlators—from a modern perspective. Later on, we generalize this concept to out-of-time order correlators, which reduce to the aforementioned out-of-time order (OTO) observables.

2.1
LSZ reduction revisited

The idea behind the LSZ reduction formula is simple: it makes precise the idea that
time-ordered products of fields

encode more information than is needed to describe in-out particle collisions characterized by
scattering amplitudes
, namely

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38092pt{{}_{\mathrm{in}}\!\bra{(j{+}1)...n}S\ket{1...j}_{\mathrm{in}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Big[\prod_{k=1}^{n}i\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{k}~\mathrm{e}^{-ip_{k}\cdot x_{k}}\big[{-}\partial^{2}_{x_{k}}{+}m_{k}^{2}\big]\Big]}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\bra{0}T(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}...\phi_{n})\ket{0}}\,.

(38)

Indeed, because
\big[{-}\partial^{2}_{x}{+}m^{2}\big]\phi(x)=0
for asymptotic (free) fields, the physical content of (
38
) is that the S-matrix
projects out
(or “amputates”) such fields from the time-ordered product. This means that only terms with a pole of the form
p^{2}+m^{2}=0
in momentum space ultimately contribute to the scattering amplitude. For what follows, it is useful to revisit
why
(
38
) holds from a modern perspective.

The first thing we do is to express the right-hand side of (
38
) in terms of the currents defined by

T(j(x)\cdots)\equiv i(-\partial_{x}^{2}+m^{2})T(\phi(x)\cdots)\,.

(39)

Given this definition, the right-hand side of (
38
) reduces to the Fourier transformed (momentum space) time-ordered correlator of currents

\begin{split}\text{RHS of~\eqref{eq:LSZamp}}&={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Big[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{k}~\mathrm{e}^{-ip_{k}\cdot x_{k}}}\Big]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bra{0}T(j_{1}j_{2}...j_{n})\ket{0}}\\
&={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bra{0}T(j(p_{1})j(p_{2})...j(p_{n}))\ket{0}}\,.\end{split}

(40)

From there, the key observation is that the on-shell limit of the currents is a total derivative. In fact, an elementary computation shows that

j(p)\equiv\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x\,\mathrm{e}^{-ip{\cdot}x}j(x)\xrightarrow{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{on-shell:}}}{{p^{2}\to-m^{2}}}}\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-ip{\cdot}x}\ (-i\partial^{\mu}_{x}+p^{\mu})\phi(x)\right]\,.

(41)

Now, we observe that the Fourier phase
\mathrm{e}^{-ip\cdot x}
oscillates rapidly as
x\to\infty
, except possibly along the direction of the external particles:
x^{\mu}\propto\pm p^{\mu}
. Along such directions, particles are eventually detected by a detector infinitely far in the future (or past), which is only possible if
constructive interference
occurs between the rapidly oscillatory Fourier phase and the phase of the field

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-ip{\cdot}x}\phi=\text{\emph{non}-oscillatory quantity along $x^{\mu}\propto\pm p^{\mu}$}\,.

(42)

In other words, (
42
) is the minimal condition that ensures non-zero asymptotic measurements (
j(p)\neq 0
). Thus, the on-shell current in (
41
) reduces to non-trivial surface terms (which
defines
for us the
a,b,a^{\dagger}
and
b^{\dagger}
introduced earlier)
only
along the trajectory of the particle. This is schematically summarized as follows:

\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#AD1737} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#AD1737} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 269.82 -260.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{196.12546pt}{-203.23543pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 271.38 -281.22)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}}}$}}\hskip-28.45274pt\leadsto\lim_{p^{2}\to-m^{2}}j(p)\equiv\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}b-a\,,&p^{0}>0\,,\\
a^{\dagger}-b^{\dagger}\,,&p^{0}<0\,.\end{array}\right.

(43)

The cartoon on the left illustrates the asymptotic measurements made in the far past and future (
a,a^{\dagger}
and
b,b^{\dagger}
, respectively) and emphasizes that they follow from constructive interference (see (
42
)) at late times along the direction of particles; along other directions, destructive interference occurs and no measurement is made. (The dotted lines show light-cone axes centered around the collision region at finite time.)
The sign of
p^{0}
determines which case in (
43
) is applicable.

The LSZ reduction formula of a time-ordered correlator easily follows: apply (
43
) to each current then follow the
T
-ordering instruction to bring all

b
and
b^{\dagger}
to the left of the
a
and
a^{\dagger}
(assuming here that
p_{i}^{0}<0
for
i\leqslant j
)

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38092pt\bra{0}T(j_{1}(p_{1})j_{2}(p_{2})...j_{n}(p_{n}))\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\bra{0}T(a_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots a_{j}^{\dagger}b_{j+1}\cdots b_{n})\ket{0}

(44)

\displaystyle=\bra{0}b_{j+1}\cdots b_{n}a_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots a_{j}^{\dagger}\ket{0}

\displaystyle={{}_{\mathrm{in}}\!\bra{(j{+}1)...n}S\ket{1...j}_{\mathrm{in}}}\,.

Here we have also used the fact that
a|0\rangle=0=\bra{0}b^{\dagger}
to eliminate all terms with an
a
or
b^{\dagger}
in (
43
). Note that this argument (and the LSZ reduction formula itself, as far as we understand) requires non-forward kinematics:
(p_{i}+p_{j})^{\mu}\neq 0
for each pair.

For out-of-time-ordered observables (which we study next), we must generally keep all the terms in (
43
). This leads to generalized LSZ reduction formulae!

2.2
Generalized reduction formulae

As reviewed above, reduction formulae connect scattering amplitudes with the on-shell limit of amputated Green functions. Below, we illustrate how generalized reduction formulae can be used to reveal non-trivial abstract features of either time-ordered or out-of-time-ordered (OTO) observables (such as analyticity). In this chapter, we examine how applying LSZ-like reductions to generic OTO correlators (OTOC) reveals a wide range of asymptotic observables. We do so from two perspectives: from an algebraic point of view (involving formal manipulations of various time ordering operators) and visually using the Schwinger–Keldysh formalism.

2.2.1
OTOC: definitions and generalities

The type of correlators we wish to reduce will always be expressible in terms of products of
time-ordered products
of the following form (introduced in the 1960s by Ruelle and Araki–Burgoyne
[
15
,
16
]
)

\langle 0|T(\phi_{1}...\phi_{i_{1}})T(\phi_{i_{1}+1}...\phi_{i_{2}})...T(\phi_{i_{n-1}+1}...\phi_{i_{n}})|0\rangle\,,

(45)

where
\phi_{n}=\phi(x_{n})
is a local operator (field)
1
1

1

Note that the notation is abused here; the fields are allowed to be of different nature, which is not apparent as we suppressed this label.
and
T(\phi_{1}...\phi_{n})
is the conventional time-ordered product of
n
local operators

\begin{split}T(\phi_{1}...\phi_{n})&=\sum_{\sigma\in\text{S}_{n}}\theta(x_{\sigma(1)}^{0}>x_{\sigma(2)}^{0}>...>x_{\sigma(n)}^{0})\epsilon(\sigma)\phi_{\sigma(1)}\phi_{\sigma(2)}...\phi_{\sigma(n)}\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\text{S}_{n}}\Big[\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\theta(x_{\sigma(m)}^{0}>x_{\sigma(m+1)}^{0})\Big]\epsilon(\sigma)\phi_{\sigma(1)}\phi_{\sigma(2)}...\phi_{\sigma(n)}\,,\end{split}

(46)

where
\text{S}_{n}
denotes the symmetric group of degree
n
and

\epsilon(\sigma)=\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}1&\text{bosonic operators}\\
\text{sign}(\sigma)&\text{fermionic operators}\end{array}\right.\,.

(47)

For example, for the bosonic two-point function, we get the familiar expression

T(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})=\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\theta(x_{1}^{0}>x_{2}^{0})+\phi_{2}\phi_{1}\theta(x_{2}^{0}>x_{1}^{0})\,.

(48)

Interestingly, linear combinations of products of
T
’s span other useful and physical objects such as
anti-time-ordered products

\overline{T}(...)
,
retarded products

R_{i}(...)
and
advanced products

A_{i}(...)
. At
n
-point, the former is simply defined by reversing the inequalities in (
46
), while the second and third are defined as follows

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359ptR_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}0}}({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}}\phi_{1}...\phi_{n})

\displaystyle{=}\sum_{\sigma\in\text{S}_{n}}\theta({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}x_{0}^{0}}{>}x_{\sigma(1)}^{0}{>}{...}{>}x_{\sigma(n)}^{0})\underbracket{\big[\big[{...}\big[\big[}_{(n{-}1)\text{-fold}}\hskip-5.69046pt{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}},\phi_{\sigma(1)}\big],\phi_{\sigma(2)}\big],{...}\big],\phi_{\sigma(n)}\big]\,,

(49a)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359ptA_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}0}}({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}}\phi_{1}...\phi_{n})

\displaystyle{=}\sum_{\sigma\in\text{S}_{n}}\theta({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}x_{0}^{0}}{<}x_{\sigma(1)}^{0}{<}...{<}x_{\sigma(n)}^{0})\big[\phi_{\sigma(n)},\big[...,\big[\phi_{\sigma(2)},\big[\phi_{\sigma(1)},{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}}\hskip-5.69046pt\underbracket{\big]\big]{...}\big]\big]}_{(n{-}1)\text{-fold}}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\,},

(49b)

with the special case
R_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}0}}({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}})={\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}}
. The
A
-product is like the
R
product with commutators nested in the second entry rather than in the first one, and with the inequalities in the
\theta
-functions reversed.

Importantly, the
R
-product (as well as the
A
-product) is symmetric in
\{1,2,{...},n\}
, but one field
{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}}
is singled out as the one with the latest time; it is
pinned
in the future of everyone. That is,

R_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}0}}({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{0}}\phi_{1}...\phi_{n})=0\qquad\text{ if }{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}x_{0}^{0}}<\max(x_{1}^{0},x_{2}^{0},...,x_{n}^{0})\,.

(50)

{mdexample}

To digest these definitions, let us look at some low-
n
examples:

\begin{split}R_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}2}}({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{2}}\phi_{1})&=R_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}2}}(\phi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{2}})=[{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{2}},\phi_{1}]\theta({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}x_{2}^{0}}>x_{1}^{0})\,,\\
R_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}3}}({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{3}}\phi_{2}\phi_{1})&=R_{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}3}}(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{3}})=[[{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{3}},\phi_{2}],\phi_{1}]\theta({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}x_{3}^{0}}>x_{2}^{0})\theta({\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}x_{2}^{0}}>x_{1}^{0})+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,.\end{split}

(51)

Note that both (
46
) and (
49a
) involve the sum over
(n-1)!
terms. Above, we indicated that both the time reversal of
(
46
)
and (
49a
) can be expressed solely in terms of standard time-ordered products. This extends to any number of fields, as we can show by a
\theta
-functions bookkeeping. Examining the simple case of
n=2
is sufficient to understand why. Indeed, introducing the shorthand notation

\theta_{ij}=\theta(x_{i}^{0}>x_{j}^{0})\,,

(52)

we have

\begin{split}\overline{T}(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})&=\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\theta_{21}+\phi_{2}\phi_{1}\theta_{12}\\
&=\phi_{1}\phi_{2}(\theta_{21}+\theta_{12})-\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\theta_{12}+\phi_{2}\phi_{1}(\theta_{12}+\theta_{21})-\phi_{2}\phi_{1}\theta_{21}\\
&=\phi_{1}\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\theta_{12}+\phi_{2}\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\phi_{1}\theta_{21}\\
&=T(\phi_{1})T(\phi_{2})+T(\phi_{2})T(\phi_{1})-T(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})\,,\end{split}

(53)

and

\begin{split}R_{1}(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})&=[\phi_{1},\phi_{2}]\theta_{12}=\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\theta_{12}-\phi_{2}\phi_{1}\theta_{12}\\
&=\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\theta_{12}-\phi_{2}\phi_{1}(\theta_{12}+\theta_{21})+\phi_{2}\phi_{1}\theta_{21}\\
&=T(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})-T(\phi_{2})T(\phi_{1})\,.\end{split}

(54)

We emphasize once more that such relations generalize to any number of points and are invertible. Consequently, replacing
T
by
\overline{T}
or
R
in (
45
) would generate the same linear span of correlators.

In the following, we revisit the Schwinger–Keldysh formalism, as described in the contribution
[
17
]
. This formalism provides a useful visual approach to understanding OTOCs.

2.2.2
Schwinger–Keldysh formalism

In order to better understand OTOCs and their evaluation in many situations, it is useful to turn to a path-integral representation. This is precisely what
Schwinger–Keldysh formalism
(SK) provides. The idea behind this formalism is quite simple:

This approach allows us to write down any combination
of anti- and time-ordered products by linking the corresponding Lorentzian (real) time axes
by infinitesimal Euclidean (imaginary) time shifts. Ultimately, this results in a path integral that features numerous time-folds that oscillate between the past and the future.

{mdexample}

To illustrate this in the context of correlators, we can consider the following seven-field correlator represented by a path integral over a Schwinger–Keldysh contour with three timefolds (I, II, and III):

\displaystyle\langle 0|\,\underbracket{T(FG)}_{\text{fold III}}\,\underbracket{\overline{T}(CDE)}_{\text{fold II}}\,\underbracket{T(AB)}_{\text{fold I}}\,|0\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\langle 0|\mathcal{C}(A^{\mathrm{I}}B^{\mathrm{I}}C^{\mathrm{II}}D^{\mathrm{II}}E^{\mathrm{II}}F^{\mathrm{III}}G^{\mathrm{III}})|0\rangle

(55)

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

The
contour-ordering symbol

\mathcal{C}
exemplified here is a natural generalization of the time-ordering symbol. The superscript on an operator labels on which timefold it is inserted. The total action includes the contribution from past directed branches with a minus sign (in our convention, the even ones:
\mathrm{II}
,
\mathrm{IV}
, …) since
\text{d}t<0
, such that
\mathrm{e}^{iS_{\mathcal{C}}}=\mathrm{e}^{iS^{\mathrm{I}}-iS^{\mathrm{II}}+iS^{\mathrm{III}}-iS^{\mathrm{IV}}+\ldots}
.
The path integral measure is simply a product of the usual one:
\int\mathcal{D}\phi=\int\mathcal{D}\phi^{\mathrm{I}}\int\mathcal{D}\phi^{\mathrm{II}}\cdots
.

{mdexample}

(Path integral for retarded products)
Let us consider what kind of formula we might expect for retarded functions
by considering the simplest case of
n=2
:

\langle 0|R_{2}(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})|0\rangle=\langle 0|[\phi_{2},\phi_{1}]\theta_{21}|0\rangle=\langle 0|(\phi_{2}\phi_{1}-\phi_{1}\phi_{2})\theta_{21}|0\rangle\,.

(56)

If we were to represent the two terms in parentheses, ignoring the
\theta
-function, one would obtain the following pictures

I

1
2

and

I

2
1

(57)

However, it is clear that the second contour cannot accurately describe (
56
) precisely due to the presence of the
\theta
function, which requires that
1
is in the past of
2
. A straightforward and correct solution to this problem is to
superpose
both contours and to
fold
them as follows

\begin{split}\hskip-12.80365pt\langle 0|R_{2}(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})|0\rangle{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{106.175pt}{-57.7361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 146.91 -79.89)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 103.78 -103.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{76.175pt}{-80.50832pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 105.4 -111.4)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}}}$}&{=}\langle 0|\mathcal{C}(\phi_{2}(\phi_{1}^{\text{I}}{-}\phi_{1}^{\text{II}}))|0\rangle\\
&\equiv\langle 0|\mathcal{C}(\phi_{2}\phi_{1}^{\text{diff}})|0\rangle\,,\end{split}

(58)

where
\phi_{k}^{{\text{diff}}}(x)\equiv\phi_{k}^{\mathrm{I}}(x)-\phi_{k}^{\mathrm{II}}(x)
. Note that the minus sign comes from the commutator and that the role of the
\mathcal{C}
-operation is simply to bring any
\phi^{\text{II}}
to the left of
\phi^{\text{I}}
.

At
n
-point, we replace the commutator in (
56
) by the nested commutator in (
49a
) and repeat the same exercise to get the
n
-point retarded product in terms of the
n
-point correlators of field differences, namely

\begin{split}R_{0}(\phi_{0}\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n-1})&\equiv\mathcal{C}(\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{I}}\phi_{1}^{{\text{diff}}}\cdots\phi_{n-1}^{{\text{diff}}})\\

}}}}}}$}\,.\end{split}

(59)

Note that the relative positions of the fields
1,\ldots,n-1
with respect to each other on the timefolds are arbitrary; the key point is that
\phi_{0}
has to be the farthest in the future; otherwise we get zero. For advanced products,
\phi_{0}
is simply positioned in the past relative to all other fields.

Let us now illustrate how Cutkosky-like cutting rules arise from the Schwinger–Keldysh formalism.

{mdexample}

The
largest time equation

[
18
]

\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\sigma\in P(k,n{-}k)}\overline{T}(\phi_{\sigma(1)}...\phi_{\sigma(k)})T(\phi_{\sigma(k+1)}...\phi_{\sigma(n)})=0\,,

(60)

(where
P(k,n{-}k)
denotes the set of partitions of
n
labels into two sets of size
k
and
n-k
) which reflects the fact that unitarity cuts are not independent of each other, takes a very simple form in the SK formalism

\langle 0|\,\mathcal{C}(\phi_{1}^{{\text{diff}}}\phi_{2}^{{\text{diff}}}\cdots\phi_{n}^{{\text{diff}}})\,|0\rangle=0\,.

(61)

This equation is manifestly true thanks to the boundary condition

\lim_{x^{0}\to\infty}\phi^{\mathrm{I}}-\phi^{\mathrm{II}}=0\,,

(62)

where the timefolds meet. It is not difficult to check that (
61
) is equivalent to (
60
). This is particularly easy to check explicitly on low
n
cases, e.g., for
n=2

(
60
)

\displaystyle\leadsto(-1)^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})}{+}(-1)^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\overline{T}(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})}{+}(-1)^{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\overline{T}(\phi_{1})T(\phi_{2})}{+}(-1)^{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\overline{T}(\phi_{2})T(\phi_{1})}=0\,,

(
61
)

\displaystyle\leadsto\langle 0|\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\phi_{1}^{\text{I}}\phi_{2}^{\text{I}}}{-}{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\phi_{2}^{\text{II}}\phi_{1}^{\text{I}}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\phi_{1}^{\text{II}}\phi_{2}^{\text{I}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}\phi_{1}^{\text{II}}\phi_{1}^{\text{II}}}\,|0\rangle=0\,.

(63)

We conclude our discussion of the Schwinger–Keldysh formalism’s generalities by presenting some illustrative examples of how various products of
T
,
\bar{T}
and
R
-products can be realized on multifold contours.

{mdexample}

\textstyle R_{F}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}F}{\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.85pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{{$G$}}\hss}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\}R_{C}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.71,0.16}C}{\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.85pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{{$D$}}\hss}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.85pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{{$E$}}\hss}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\}R_{A}\{{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}A}{\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.85pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{{$B$}}\hss}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\}
II

III

IV

V

VI

On the top left, we have a product of time-ordered products
T
, while on the top right, we have a product of anti-time-ordered products
\bar{T}
. The cartoon on the bottom left represents a mixed product of time- and anti-time-ordered products, while the cartoon on the bottom right represents a product of retarded products
R
. As before, time goes from right to left in each panel.

The takeaway point here is that the set of operators spanned by multi-timefold Schwinger–Keldysh correlators such as (
55
) is, after
\theta
-functions bookkeeping, in 1-1 correspondence with the set identified in (
45
). Next, we will extend the familiar LSZ reduction formula revisited earlier to the OTOC we just introduced.

2.2.3
Making manifest analyticity properties

In what follows, we consider a few examples of interesting reduction formulae. As a warm-up, we consider once more conventional time-ordered amplitudes with the aim of establishing the so-called
optical theorem
/discontinuity/unitarity formula for
2\leftarrow 2
amplitudes. Later, applications at higher multiplicity will be discussed.

{mdexample}

(Four-point amplitudes and discontinuities)

In order to achieve our first goal, let us recall a useful implication of the stability condition

Sa^{\dagger}\ket{0}=a^{\dagger}\ket{0}=b^{\dagger}\ket{0}=Sb^{\dagger}\ket{0}\,,

(65)

following from the axioms introduced earlier, namely that

{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}b_{2}^{\dagger}a_{1}^{\dagger}}\ket{0}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}b_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}}\ket{0}=b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger}\ket{0}\,.

(66)

Using these, we can prove a useful intermediate result: in
34\leftarrow 12
kinematics, we have

\begin{split}T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle&=T((a_{1}^{\dagger}-b_{1}^{\dagger})(a_{2}^{\dagger}-b_{2}^{\dagger}))|0\rangle\\
&=T(a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}-a_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger}-b_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}+b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger})|0\rangle\\
&=(a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}-{\color[rgb]{0.68,0,0}b_{2}^{\dagger}a_{1}^{\dagger}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}b_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}}+b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger})|0\rangle\\
&=(a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}-b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger})|0\rangle\\
&=\ket{12}-S^{\dagger}a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}S|0\rangle\\
&=(\mathbbm{1}-S^{\dagger})\ket{12}\,.\end{split}

(67)

We see from this simple calculation that
T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle
simply picks up the interacting part of
S
(it vanishes when the particles do not interact)! This identity can, of course, be applied to any state. For example, one can obtain the fully connected part of the amplitude as follows

\begin{split}{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle&={}_{\text{in\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}S(\mathbbm{1}-S^{\dagger})\ket{12}\\
&={}_{\text{in\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}(S-\mathbbm{1})\ket{12}\\
&=i\mathcal{M}_{34\leftarrow 12}\,\end{split}

(68)

More interestingly, we can consider the correlator
\langle 0|\overline{T}(j_{3}j_{4})T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle
. On the one hand, we can insert a complete basis of asymptotic states via the resolution of identity

\mathbbm{1}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\ket{X}_{\mathrlap{\text{in}}}{\hskip-11.38092pt\phantom{\bra{X}}}{}_{\mathrlap{\text{in}}\hskip 4.26773pt}\bra{X}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\ket{X}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}}{\hskip-5.69046pt\phantom{\bra{X}}}{}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}\hskip 9.95863pt}\bra{X}\,,

(69)

according to

\displaystyle\langle 0|\overline{T}(j_{3}j_{4})T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\langle 0|\overline{T}(j_{3}j_{4})\ket{X}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}}{\hskip-5.69046pt\phantom{\bra{X}}}{}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}\hskip 9.95863pt}\bra{X}T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\bra{34}(\mathbbm{1}-S)\ket{X}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}}{\hskip-5.69046pt\phantom{\bra{X}}}{}_{\mathrlap{\text{out}}\hskip 9.95863pt}\bra{X}(\mathbbm{1}-S^{\dagger})\ket{12}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\bra{34}(\mathbbm{1}-S)S^{\dagger}\ket{X}_{\mathrlap{\text{in}}}{\hskip-11.38092pt\phantom{\bra{X}}}{}_{\mathrlap{\text{in}}\hskip 4.26773pt}\bra{X}S(\mathbbm{1}-S^{\dagger})\ket{12}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\mathcal{M}_{34\leftarrow X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{M}_{X\leftarrow 12}\,.

On the other hand, we can directly compute
\langle 0|\overline{T}(j_{3}j_{4})T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle
using (
67
) twice:

\begin{split}\langle 0|\overline{T}(j_{3}j_{4})T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle&=(\langle 0|\overline{T}(j_{3}j_{4}))(T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle)\\
&=(\langle 0|(a_{3}a_{4}-b_{3}b_{4}))((a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}-b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger})|0\rangle)\,.\end{split}

(71)

Dropping the forward terms and using

(\mathcal{M}_{A\leftarrow B})^{\ast}=\langle A|S|B\rangle^{\ast}=\langle B|S^{\dagger}|A\rangle=\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{B\leftarrow A}\,,

(72)

we are left with

\begin{split}\langle 0|\overline{T}(j_{3}j_{4})T(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle&=-\langle 0|a_{3}a_{4}b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger}|0\rangle-\langle 0|b_{3}b_{4}a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}|0\rangle\\
&=-(\langle 0|b_{1}b_{2}a_{3}^{\dagger}a_{4}^{\dagger}|0\rangle)^{\dagger}-\langle 0|b_{3}b_{4}a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}|0\rangle\\
&=i\mathcal{M}_{34\leftarrow 12}^{\dagger}-i\mathcal{M}_{34\leftarrow 12}\,.\end{split}

(73)

Comparing both sides of (
70
) and (
73
) gives the optical theorem.

{mdexample}

(The role of
R
-products in axiomatic field theory)

Let us first illustrate why
R
-products are useful, again, with conventional time-ordered
2\leftarrow 2
scattering amplitudes. A first useful identity can be derived from the decomposition of the 2-point
R
-product in terms of
T
-products where both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
have negative energies:

\begin{split}R_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})&=T(j_{1}j_{2})-T(j_{1})T(j_{2})\\
&=T((a_{1}^{\dagger}-b_{1}^{\dagger})(a_{2}^{\dagger}-b_{2}^{\dagger}))-(a_{1}^{\dagger}-b_{1}^{\dagger})(a_{2}^{\dagger}-b_{2}^{\dagger})\\
&=(\cancel{a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}}-b_{2}^{\dagger}a_{1}^{\dagger}-\cancel{b_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}}+\cancel{b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger}})-(\cancel{a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}}-a_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger}-\cancel{b_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}}+\cancel{b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger}})\\
&=-[b_{2}^{\dagger},a_{1}^{\dagger}]\,.\end{split}

(74)

Note that physically, the terms involving
b^{\dagger}_{1}
were expected to cancel because
j_{1}
must be in the past lightcone of
j_{2}
and therefore it cannot be an “out” measurement. Consequently, particle 1 cannot reach future infinity (it returns to past infinity). From there, an elementary computation using (
66
) shows that

\begin{split}{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}R_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle&=-{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}[b_{2}^{\dagger},a_{1}^{\dagger}]|0\rangle\\
&=-{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}b_{2}^{\dagger}a_{1}^{\dagger}|0\rangle+{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}a_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}^{\dagger}|0\rangle\\
&=-\underbracket{{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}b_{2}^{\dagger}b_{1}^{\dagger}|0\rangle}_{\text{Forward!}}+{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}|0\rangle\\
&={}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}a_{1}^{\dagger}a_{2}^{\dagger}|0\rangle=\bra{34}S\ket{12}\,.\end{split}

(75)

Thus, the conventional time-ordered scattering amplitude is also equal to the on-shell limit of some
R
products.
What is interesting is that the
R
-product is supported over
x_{2}^{0}>x_{1}^{0}
. This guarantees that its Fourier transform

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}(p_{1},p_{2})=\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{1}\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-i(p_{1}\cdot x_{1}+p_{2}\cdot x_{2})}{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}R_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle\,,\end{split}

(76)

is analytic/converges in a certain domain, in which equal positive timelike imaginary parts are added to
p_{2}^{\mu}
and
-p_{1}^{\mu}
. Indeed, under the relabeling
x_{1}=x_{2}+y
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}(p_{1},p_{2})&=\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}y\mathrm{e}^{-ip_{1}\cdot y}\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-i(p_{1}+p_{2})\cdot x_{2}}{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}R_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle\,,\end{split}

(77)

x_{2}^{0}>x_{1}^{0}\implies y^{0}=x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}<0\implies y\in V^{-}\,.

(78)

Now, because
x_{2}^{0}>x_{1}^{0}
, we must have
y^{0}=x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}<0
and we conclude that
y
is in the past lightcone
V^{-}
(past pointing). This means that the Fourier transform only converges provided that the first exponential decays, while the second oscillates. This happens precisely when

\text{Re}(-iy\cdot p_{1})=y\cdot\text{Im}(p_{1})<0\quad\text{while}\quad p_{1}+p_{2}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}\,.

(79)

Since we work in mostly-plus signature, asking
y
and
\text{Im}(p_{1})
to be timelike separated (implying that
\text{Im}(p_{1})\in V^{-}
) is equivalent to the first condition above. Thus, (
75
) indicates that the amplitude is
at least
analytic provided
that equal and opposite timelike imaginary parts are added to
p_{1}^{\mu}
and
p_{2}^{\mu}
.

Of course, one could also represent the amplitude using the advanced product

\begin{split}A_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})=[j_{1},j_{2}]\theta_{12}=-[j_{2},j_{1}]\theta_{12}=-[j_{2},j_{1}]+R_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})\,.\end{split}

(80)

Indeed, since it differs from the retarded product only by a commutator (which actually vanishes in this kinematic) involving no
\theta
-function,
we have

{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}A_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle={}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}R_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle-{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}\underbracket{[j_{2},j_{1}]|0\rangle}_{=0}=\eqref{eq:ampRet1}\,.

(81)

Therefore, taking the Fourier transform of
{}_{\text{out\hskip-2.84544pt}}\bra{34}A_{2}(j_{1}j_{2})|0\rangle
, one would obtain a similar set of conditions to that in (
79
), but with the inequality sign reversed.
By “edge-of-the-wedge” type arguments, the equality of retarded and advanced representations suggests that the amplitude is analytic in a larger domain where equal and opposite imaginary parts are indeed added to
p_{1}^{\mu}
and
p_{2}^{\mu}
, but with the timelike requirement relaxed—as required to be on-shell.

The existence of multiple reduction formulae for the same quantity has significant implications. In particular, they are often central in axiomatic proofs that scattering amplitudes are analytic, for example, in a neighborhood of the mass shell
[
9
,
10
,
19
]
. The argument also generalizes to
m\leftarrow n
scattering and has been used to demonstrate that amplitudes near the mass shell are essentially a finite sum of analytic functions
[
11
]
, with a single function sufficing for
2\leftarrow 2
scattering. Moreover, while there exist only two distinct asymptotic observables at four points, namely
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
, these can be written in terms of a significantly greater number of correlation functions which enjoy distinct analyticity properties. We expect that a solid understanding of these correlation functions will add fruitful insight into the analytic properties of amplitudes.

Another reduction formula can be obtained similarly to (
75
) by inserting a retarded product between
one in and out
single
-particle states. For example, focusing again on
34\leftarrow 12
kinematics, inserting

\begin{split}R_{3}\{j_{3}j_{2}\}&=T(j_{3}j_{2}){-}T(j_{2})T(j_{3})\\
&=T((b_{3}{-}a_{3})(a_{2}^{\dagger}{-}b_{2}^{\dagger})){-}(a_{2}^{\dagger}{-}b_{2}^{\dagger})(b_{3}{-}a_{3})\\
&=[b_{3},a_{2}^{\dagger}]\,,\end{split}

(82)

we get (after using stability once more)

\langle 4|R_{3}\{j_{3}j_{2}\}|1\rangle=\langle 4|b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}|1\rangle=\langle 4|a_{3}Sa^{\dagger}_{2}|1\rangle=\langle 34|S|12\rangle\,.

(83)

This formula is relevant to analyticity at fixed-
t
and crossing symmetry through the upper-half
s
-plane
[
10
]
. When the energies of particles
2
and
3
are flipped

\begin{split}R_{3}\{j_{3}j_{2}\}&=T(j_{3}j_{2})-T(j_{2})T(j_{3})\\
&=T((a^{\dagger}_{3}-b_{3}^{\dagger})(b_{2}-a_{2}))-(b_{2}-a_{2})(a_{3}^{\dagger}-b_{3}^{\dagger})\\
&=[b_{3}^{\dagger},a_{2}]\,,\end{split}

(84)

for the same correlator, we get

\langle 4|R_{3}\{j_{3}j_{2}\}|1\rangle=-\langle 4|a_{2}b^{\dagger}_{3}|1\rangle=-\langle 4|a_{2}S^{\dagger}a^{\dagger}_{3}|1\rangle=-\langle 42|S^{\dagger}|13\rangle\,.

(85)

Thus, it reduces to
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
instead of
\mathcal{M}
. This is essentially the
2\to 2
case of
crossing symmetry
in (
28
) (see (
17
))!
The general case of (
28
) is obtained in a similar way by not using the stability condition.

We now describe for the first time a higher point application of retarded products.

{mdexample}

(Higher point discontinuities and Steinmann relations)
In
234\leftarrow 12
kinematics, consider the on-shell limit:

\begin{split}-\langle 0|R_{5}(j_{5}j_{4})R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle=~?\,\end{split}

(86)

Its reduction in terms of familiar time-ordered objects combines (
74
) (but without daggers) and (
82
). Indeed, using stability
\bra{0}a=\bra{0}b
once again, we have

\displaystyle-\langle 0|R_{5}(j_{5}j_{4})R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle 0|(-[b_{5},a_{4}])([b_{3},a^{\dagger}_{2}])a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 0|a_{5}a_{4}b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle-\langle 0|b_{4}b_{5}b_{3}a^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle

(87)

\displaystyle=\langle 0|a_{5}a_{4}S^{\dagger}a_{3}Sa^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle-\langle 0|a_{4}a_{5}a_{3}Sa^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle\,.

Now, we can make the following replacements:
S^{\dagger}=\mathbbm{1}-iT^{\dagger}
and
S\mapsto iT
. The asymmetry between
S
and
S^{\dagger}
has a simple interpretation:
R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})
requires particle
3
to be in the future lightcone of particle
2
; thus particles
1
and
2
must interact together
before
particle
3
is emitted, since all particles are stable and cannot freely radiate. Putting everything together, dropping the forward terms, and inserting a complete basis of asymptotic states
X
, we have

\begin{split}-\langle 0|R_{5}(j_{5}j_{4})R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle&=i(-i)\langle 0|a_{5}a_{4}T^{\dagger}a_{3}Ta^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle\\
&=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sum$\cr$\displaystyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sum$\cr$\textstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptstyle\int$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\int$\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{X}\langle 0|a_{5}a_{4}T^{\dagger}\ket{X}\bra{X}a_{3}Ta^{\dagger}_{2}a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle\\
&=\raisebox{-30.00005pt}{
\hbox to148.78pt{\vbox to55.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.9342pt\lower-34.68576pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.81 L -47.24 11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-42.6012pt}{5.63591pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -58.95 7.8)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -11.81 L -47.24 -11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

&\equiv{\text{Cut}}_{45}\bm{\delta}_{5}\,.\end{split}

(88)

Thus, the correlator (
86
) is just a particular way of writing a unitarity cut. It turns out that this representation has a very interesting feature. To see it, let us examine a bit its Fourier transform

\int\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{5}\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{4}\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{3}\text{d}^{\mathrm{D}}x_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-i(p_{5}\cdot x_{5}+p_{4}\cdot x_{4}+p_{3}\cdot x_{3}+p_{2}\cdot x_{2})}\langle 0|R_{5}(j_{5}j_{4})R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle\,,

(89)

and perform, say, the constant timelike shifts
x_{3}\mapsto x_{3}+\xi
and
x_{4}\mapsto x_{4}+\xi
. Now, physically, if particles
3
and
4
are measured by a detector asymptotically far in the future (
\xi\to\infty
), we expect some
s_{34}
-channel non-analyticity in the momentum space representation (
89
).

However, because of the explicit dependence of the integrand on
R_{5}(j_{5}j_{4})
and
R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})
, the
\xi
-range is constrained as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=1} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 314.96 -150.33 C 314.96 -149.06 315.99 -148.03 317.26 -148.03 C 318.53 -148.03 319.56 -149.06 319.56 -150.33 C 319.56 -151.6 318.53 -152.63 317.26 -152.63 C 315.99 -152.63 314.96 -151.6 314.96 -150.33 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0080FF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#0080FF} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=1} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=1} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{213.7156pt}{-110.04776pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 295.72 -152.27)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=1} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 305.62 -177.31 C 305.62 -176.04 306.65 -175.01 307.92 -175.01 C 309.19 -175.01 310.22 -176.04 310.22 -177.31 C 310.22 -178.58 309.19 -179.61 307.92 -179.61 C 306.65 -179.61 305.62 -178.58 305.62 -177.31 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#AD1737} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#AD1737} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=1} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#AD1737} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#AD1737} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=1} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=1} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{227.42828pt}{-129.54776pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 314.69 -179.26)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}}}$}

(90)

In other words, the integrand has support only for

\displaystyle\infty\geqslant x_{3}^{\pm}-x_{2}^{\pm}

\displaystyle>0\mapsto\xi^{\pm}>x_{2}^{\pm}-x_{3}^{\pm}\geqslant-\infty\,,

(91a)

\displaystyle\infty\geqslant x_{5}^{\pm}-x_{4}^{\pm}

\displaystyle>0\mapsto\infty\geqslant x_{5}^{\pm}-x_{4}^{\pm}>\xi^{\pm}\,,

(91b)

such that

\infty\geqslant x_{5}^{\pm}-x_{4}^{\pm}>\xi^{\pm}>x_{2}^{\pm}-x_{3}^{\pm}\geqslant-\infty\,.

(92)

Furthermore, since
\xi
is timelike, we have a constraint on
\mathbf{\xi}_{\perp}

\xi^{2}=-\xi^{+}\xi^{-}+\mathbf{\xi}_{\perp}^{2}<0\implies-\sqrt{\xi^{+}\xi^{-}}<|\mathbf{\xi}_{\perp}|<\sqrt{\xi^{+}\xi^{-}}\,.

(93)

Conditions (
92
) and (
93
) put together prohibit past and future infinite timelike translations (i.e., limits
|\xi|\to\infty
). This indicates that we should not expect any
s_{34}
-channel singularity. In other words, we just argued that

\text{Disc}_{s_{34}}\text{Cut}_{45}=0\,.

(94)

This is an example of the so-called
Steinmann relations
. Of course, going through a similar reduction argument on the correlator
\langle 0|R_{4}(j_{5}j_{4})R_{3}(j_{3}j_{2})a^{\dagger}_{1}|0\rangle
, one could similarly argue that

\text{Disc}_{s_{35}}\text{Cut}_{45}=0\,.

(95)

As before, this discussion suggests that the existence of multiple reduction formulae is central in revealing the full analytic properties of amplitudes.

In the last few examples we examined, the Schwinger–Keldysh framework was not used. To demonstrate how it can be used to extract quantitative details about OTOCs, we next apply it to analyze the infrared (IR) divergences of the five-point in-in observable in (
23
).

2.3
Infrared divergences of in-in QED observables from the timefold

The purpose of this section is to analyze how the Schwinger–Keldysh picture introduced above can be used to understand the infrared (IR) properties of out-of-time-ordered observables. To do so, we will focus on a particular in-in QED observable, namely the inclusive measurement (“waveform”) of a photon in the background of two electrically charged particles (which we will take to be electrons); the analogous calculation in gravity is discussed in
[
2
]
(see also
(
20
, Sec. 4)
).
Using the blob pictures introduced earlier, this observable is represented as

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.88092pt}{-82.89001pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 12.29 -114.69)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.73505pt}{-95.04124pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -10.7 -131.51)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 -118.11}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.02104pt}{-95.04124pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 5.56 -131.51)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 114.17 -118.11}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
